Hi, thank you. Well, what really pushed me to make this appointment is... it's work stuff mostly. I'm an auditor, and I keep starting these follow-up items after my audits, or even after the mentorship events I run. 

I actually really love the mentorship program, it's so rewarding to help the junior accountants. When I was starting out right after I got my degree, I had this mentor, Brenda, who was just incredible. She taught me so much, but she also made the absolute best snickerdoodle cookies. She used to bring them in on Fridays in this little blue tin. I actually tried baking some last weekend, but I think my oven runs hot because the bottoms completely burnt to a crisp, which is a shame because I bought the really expensive vanilla extract for it. 

But anyway, wait, what was I... oh, right, work. So I start these follow-ups with every intention of finishing them. I really do. And then I'll find out, like, days later that the final email, or the review note, or the sign-off form just... never actually got done. I just leave them hanging without realizing it.

Oh, no, it's definitely not just a recent thing. It's been happening forever. Even back in elementary school, my report cards always said the exact same thing. They'd say I was bright, but I rushed past small details and talked out of turn... 

Speaking of talking out of turn, I got in so much trouble in fourth grade because I wouldn't stop talking to my best friend, Sarah. She had this amazing collection of gel pens. Remember those? The milky ones and the ones that smelled like fruit? I think the watermelon one was my absolute favorite. We used to trade them during math class, which Mr. Henderson just hated. He was so strict, he actually confiscated my whole pencil case once. I was so upset because it had my lucky eraser in it, the one shaped like a little turtle. I bought it at the book fair, and the book fairs were just the best day of the year, weren't they? I loved the posters they sold. 

But, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, school and my report cards. Yeah, the teachers always wrote that I needed constant reminders to finish my work, and I'd lose my assignments all the time. My mom actually still jokes about it. She always says I could completely ace a test, but then I'd forget to turn in the big project that was literally just sitting right there in my backpack the whole time. Backpacks were so heavy back then, too. I had this huge neon one... I don't even know how my little shoulders carried it. But anyway, yeah, it's been an issue since I was little.

Oh, definitely. Sitting still is... well, it's a real challenge. Especially at work. Like, when I'm sitting in meetings, I'm usually tapping my foot under the table, or clicking my pen constantly, or just shifting around in my chair a million times. I used to bake all the time, but now...

Oh, yeah, it's definitely not just a work thing. It bleeds into everything. At home, my husband has basically had to take over paying all the bills and handling any commitments I agree to, just because he really can't count on me to complete them reliably.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think I've ever had anything like that. I mean, my thoughts can race sometimes, but that's just my normal, you know? It's not like a special period of days where I feel on top of the world or anything. I usually need my sleep. If I don't get at least seven hours, I am just a wreck the next day. 

Speaking of sleep, we actually just bought a new mattress last weekend. It's one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box. But yeah, the foot tapping and shifting around in my chair is pretty much a constant thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these same difficulties with distractibility, forgetfulness, or restlessness affecting things outside of work, like your home life, relationships, or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, it's definitely not just a work thing. It bleeds into everything. At home, my husband has basically had to take over paying all the bills and handling any commitments I agree to, just because he really can't count on me to complete them reliably. \n\nLike, I signed us up to bring a pasta salad to the neighborhood block party last month, and I completely forgot to make it until the morning of. We ended up having to buy one from the deli at the grocery store. The deli there is usually pretty good, actually. They have this amazing macaroni salad with little cubes of cheddar cheese in it. I always try to recreate it at home, but I can never get the dressing right. I think they use a specific type of mustard or maybe some pickle juice? My grandmother used to put pickle juice in her potato salad, and it was always the biggest hit at family reunions. We used to have them at this big park in Ohio, right by this beautiful lake where we'd rent those little swan paddleboats... \n\nWait, sorry, I'm getting off track again. What did you ask? Oh, right, how it affects my life. So yeah, my husband handles all the home stuff now because I keep dropping the ball. \n\nBut honestly, it's getting really serious at work, too. Like, my mistakes and all those unfinished follow-ups I mentioned earlier? They actually affected a client review recently. It was awful. Now I'm really worrying that people are questioning whether I can actually handle the promotion I'm up for. The promotion would be amazing, though, it comes with a dedicated parking spot in the garage, and parking downtown is just a nightmare right now with all the construction on 5th street. They've had that road torn up since April, and the detour adds like twenty minutes to my commute, which just makes my mornings so incredibly stressful before I even get to my desk... but, um, anyway. Yes. It affects my home life a lot, too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had distinct periods lasting several days where you felt unusually elevated, euphoric, or irritable, needed much less sleep than usual, and had racing thoughts, separate from how you feel most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever had anything like that. I mean, my thoughts can race sometimes, but that's just my normal, you know? It's not like a special period of days where I feel on top of the world or anything. I usually need my sleep. If I don't get at least seven hours, I am just a wreck the next day. \n\nSpeaking of sleep, we actually just bought a new mattress last weekend. It's one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box. It's wild how they fit a whole king-size bed into this cardboard box. The delivery guy just left it on the porch, and my husband and I had to drag it upstairs. It was so heavy, I thought we were going to drop it down the stairs and put a hole in the drywall. We had to patch a hole in the drywall a few years ago when my nephew was visiting and he accidentally threw a baseball in the house. He felt so bad about it, but honestly, it was an accident. He's such a sweet kid, he plays on a little league team now and they actually made it to the championships this year. We went to watch him play and it was so hot out, we had to sit under this little umbrella...\n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? Oh, right, feeling euphoric or not needing sleep. No, I haven't experienced anything like that. Just my normal scattered brain, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently worrying excessively about a lot of different things—like work, health, or family—even when there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: Not really? I mean, I stress about work, obviously, because of the mistakes I'm making and the promotion, but I don't just sit around worrying about everything for no reason. \n\nMy mom is exactly like that, though. She worries about absolutely everything. She actually called me yesterday panicking because she read some article on Facebook about how non-stick frying pans are toxic. So now she's throwing out all her cookware and buying all new stainless steel. Which is so expensive! I told her she should just go to HomeGoods, they always have those nice heavy-bottomed pans in the kitchen aisle. I love HomeGoods. I went in there last week just to buy a vanilla candle and walked out with a whole new set of decorative pillows for the living room. They had these really soft velvet ones on clearance in this gorgeous mustard yellow color, and I just couldn't leave them behind. My husband just rolled his eyes when I brought them in, because we already have way too many pillows on the couch, but they really tie the room together... \n\nWait, sorry. I completely lost my train of thought again. You were asking about worrying? Yeah, no, I don't really have that problem. Just the normal stuff."

Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nOh, no, it's definitely not just a recent thing. It's been happening forever. Even back in elementary school, my report cards always said the exact same thing. They'd say I was bright, but I rushed past small details and talked out of turn... \n\nSpeaking of talking out of turn, I got in so much trouble in fourth grade because I wouldn't stop talking to my best friend, Sarah. She had this amazing collection of gel pens. Remember those? The milky ones and the ones that smelled like fruit? I think the watermelon one was my absolute favorite. We used to trade them during math class, which Mr. Henderson just hated. He was so strict, he actually confiscated my whole pencil case once. I was so upset because it had my lucky eraser in it, the one shaped like a little turtle. I bought it at the book fair, and the book fairs were just the best day of the year, weren't they? I loved the posters they sold. \n\nBut, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, school and my report cards. Yeah, the teachers always wrote that I needed constant reminders to finish my work, and I'd lose my assignments all the time. My mom actually still jokes about it. She always says I could completely ace a test, but then I'd forget to turn in the big project that was literally just sitting right there in my backpack the whole time. Backpacks were so heavy back then, too. I had this huge neon one... I don't even know how my little shoulders carried it. But anyway, yeah, it's been an issue since I was little.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
